  in a healthcare setting. Keep it simple : minimal scope, as few files and     
  layers as possible, but production level on security, privacy and test. I     
  come from NestJS and Java, I'm new to Python / Django. When I use NestJS      
  terms (controller, DTO, service, module etc), translate them to the idiomatic 
  Django / DRF equivalent and follow Django conventions, not NestJs structure.  
  Explain briefly the mapping when it's not obvious. Stack : api --> Django +   
  DRF, postgresql 18. Use the standard python tooling for dependencies, and     
  give me simple aliases for commom commands (Like npm scripts). The API must   
  expose an OpenAPI schema. app --> Vite + React + TypeScript + React           
  TypescriptCompiler + Tailwind (mobile first), Zod for client side validation, 
  Orval generating TanStack Query hooks from the OpenAPI schema (it'll be the   
  single source of truth for types and routes). Product scope (for later step   
  not now) : RESTful CRUD on patients, FHIR compliance for patient data, search 
  and filtering done client side, simple authentication and authorization.      
  We'll design the data model in a later step. Keep it simple do not anticipate 
  it now. For this step only : - docker-compose.yml running db, api folder and  
  app folder (credentials written directly in the file, documentated as a       
  tradeoff in the README). Scaffold both apps withe the bare minimum (no        
  features, modela or business logic) with a test runner on each side. README   
  at hte root: technical choides made, startup commands, front and backends     
  urls. Root CLAUDE.md: Projet context, scope, conventions and commands. Verify 
  the whole stack starts with docker compose. Show me the plan first if         
  necessary, then we'll commit everything as the initial commit.

⏺ Your repo is empty and the toolchain is there (Docker, Node 24, Python). I
  checked the current package versions. Here's the plan before I write anything.

  Plan

  Layout (kept small)
  docker-compose.yml   Makefile   README.md   CLAUDE.md   .gitignore
  api/   Dockerfile  pyproject.toml  manage.py  openapi.yaml (generated, 
  committed)
         config/ settings.py urls.py views.py wsgi.py asgi.py
         tests/ test_health.py test_schema.py test_security.py
  app/   Dockerfile  package.json  vite.config.ts  orval.config.ts  index.html
         src/ main.tsx App.tsx index.css  api/ (Orval output)  App.test.tsx

  Backend: Django 6.1, DRF 3.18, drf-spectacular, Python 3.14 in Docker
  - config/ is the Django project: settings, root URLs, WSGI. A rough NestJS
    equivalent is AppModule plus main.ts. Feature apps come later; patients/
    will be your module.
  - Dependencies: pyproject.toml (PEP 621) installed with pip in a venv. This is
    the official PyPA tooling, so there's nothing extra to install. I'll pin
    exact versions. Trade-off: transitive dependencies aren't locked, unlike
    package-lock.json. Say so if you'd rather use uv, which has a real lockfile
    but is a third-party tool.
  - Tests use pytest + pytest-django, the de facto standard. Linting and
    formatting use ruff, which plays the role of ESLint and Prettier.
  - One endpoint, GET /api/health/ (checks the DB). It isn't a feature: it gives
    Orval something to generate, and lets the React app prove the whole chain
    works (React → generated hook → Django → Postgres).
  - OpenAPI: /api/schema/ and Swagger UI at /api/docs/. The schema is exported
    to api/openapi.yaml and committed. A test fails if the file is out of date
    with the code, so it stays the single source of truth.
  - Secure by default:
    - Settings come only from environment variables. DEBUG defaults to off, and
      the app refuses to start without SECRET_KEY.
    - DRF defaults to IsAuthenticated, so every endpoint is closed unless it
      explicitly opts out (only health and schema do). The API returns JSON
      only, with no browsable API.
    - Security headers, HSTS and secure cookies are on whenever DEBUG is off.
      Django admin isn't installed, to keep the attack surface small.
    - The Docker image runs as a non-root user and serves with gunicorn. Compose
      overrides that with runserver for hot reload.

  Frontend: Vite 8, React 19 + React Compiler, TS, Tailwind 4, Zod 4, Orval 8, 
  TanStack Query 5, Vitest
  - Orval reads ../api/openapi.yaml and generates TanStack Query hooks (using
    the native fetch client, so no axios) plus Zod schemas.
  - The Vite dev server proxies /api to Django. Frontend and API share one
    origin, so there's no CORS to configure, and cookie/CSRF auth will be simple
    later.
  - The page is a mobile-first shell that shows the API health status. One
    Vitest + Testing Library test covers it.

  Docker Compose
  - Services: db (postgres:18), api and app, with bind mounts for hot reload.
  - Ports are bound to 127.0.0.1 only.
  - Credentials are written inline in the file; the README documents this as a
    trade-off.
  - Postgres 18 changed its data directory, so the volume is mounted at
    /var/lib/postgresql. The old /var/lib/postgresql/data path no longer works.

  Command aliases: a root Makefile (the closest thing to npm scripts here), 
  running inside the containers
  make up / down / logs / test / test-api / test-app / lint / format / schema
  (exports the schema, then runs Orval) / migrate / makemigrations / shell / 
  check-deploy. The app also keeps its own native npm scripts.

  Verification: docker compose up --build, then check health through the Vite
  proxy and that /api/docs/ loads, run both test suites and lint, then make the
  initial commit.

  Shall I go ahead? Tell me if you want uv instead of pip, or anything else
  changed.

✻ Churned for 1m 22s · done Friday 5:40 PM

※ recap: You're setting up a Django and React patient management app with Docker, and I've shown the scaffold plan without creating any files yet. Next step: tell me to go ahead, and say whether you want pip or uv for Python dependencies. (turn off recaps in /config)

❯ what's the diff between uv and pip ?                                          

⏺ pip is Python's official installer and comes with Python. It only installs
  packages. You combine it with:
  - venv (built in) for the isolated environment, a bit like a project-local
    node_modules
  - pyproject.toml to declare dependencies, like package.json

  It has no stable lockfile yet. pip freeze > requirements.txt captures what's
  installed, but it's a snapshot, not a resolved lock. The standard lock format
  (pylock.toml, PEP 755) exists, but pip's support for it is still experimental.

  uv is a third-party tool from Astral, the company behind ruff. One binary
  replaces pip, venv, pip-tools and pyenv. Roughly, it's the npm/pnpm of Python:

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │                 │      pip + venv       │              uv              │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Install source  │ Ships with Python     │ Separate install (brew       │
  │                 │                       │ install uv)                  │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Lockfile        │ ❌ (pins by hand or   │ ✅ uv.lock, cross-platform,  │
  │                 │ pip freeze)           │ with hashes                  │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Reproducible    │ Direct dependencies   │ ✅ uv sync --frozen, like    │
  │ installs        │ only, if you pin them │ npm ci                       │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Manages venv    │ Manual (python -m     │ Automatic                    │
  │                 │ venv .venv)           │                              │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Manages Python  │ ❌                    │ ✅ (uv python install 3.14)  │
  │ version         │                       │                              │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Speed           │ Slow                  │ 10–100× faster               │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Add a           │ Edit pyproject, then  │ uv add django, like npm      │
  │ dependency      │ pip install           │ install django               │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │                       │ ❌ (no npm-style scripts     │
  │ Script runner   │ ❌                    │ either, so the Makefile      │
  │                 │                       │ stays)                       │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Status          │ Official PyPA         │ De facto standard for new    │
  │                 │ standard              │ projects, but not official   │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  For this project: "production level" generally means reproducible builds,
  where the same transitive versions and hashes are installed in CI, Docker and
  prod. pip can't give you that natively today; uv can. The cost is one extra
  tool, and only on your machine, since Docker installs it in the image.

  My recommendation is uv. The lockfile matters for a healthcare app (supply
  chain, reproducibility), and the workflow will feel familiar from npm. Your
  project files stay standard (pyproject.toml), so switching back to pip later
  is easy.

  Shall I go with uv?

⏺ Everything is green from a clean start. Committing now.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The scaffold is committed as e278ab6 (38 files). From a clean start, make up
  brings the whole stack up healthy. The 9 backend tests and 2 frontend tests
  pass, and lint and typecheck are clean. The stack is still running.

  URLs
  - App: http://localhost:5173
  - API: http://localhost:8000/api/health/
  - Swagger: http://localhost:8000/api/docs/
  - Schema: http://localhost:8000/api/schema/

  Commands: make up, make test, make lint, make schema (after any API change)
  and make help for the rest.

  How the API contract flows. Django code generates api/openapi.yaml, which is
  committed. A test fails if it's out of date with the code. Orval reads that
  file and generates useGetHealth plus the types and Zod schemas into
  app/src/api/generated/. That folder isn't committed; it's rebuilt
  automatically before dev, test, build and typecheck. The page shows "API
  status: ok", which proves the whole chain works: React → generated hook → Vite
  proxy → Django → Postgres.

  Departures from the plan, or things to know
  - Compose project name. I named it therapieland-case because a Docker volume
    patient-management_db-data already exists on your machine with a different
    DB password. I left that volume alone.
  - Swagger UI not checked in a browser. The Chrome extension wasn't connected.
    The page returns 200, but the CSP is only enforced by browsers, so please
    open /api/docs/ once to confirm it renders.
  - One warning from make check-deploy. HSTS preload is off on purpose, since
    it's hard to undo. The README explains why.
  - CDN assets. Swagger UI was loading @latest from the CDN, so I pinned it to
    swagger-ui-dist@5.36.0.
  - No browsable API. The API returns JSON only, so DRF's HTML browsable API is
    off. Use Swagger to explore it.
  - No authentication configured yet. The API is deny-by-default with no login
    mechanism, so any new endpoint returns 403 until the auth step. That's
    intended. Public endpoints are listed in api/tests/test_security.py, and a
    test fails if one is added without updating that list.
  - Smaller than planned. I dropped asgi.py and Django admin. I also added a
    healthcheck on the app container so make up waits until Vite is actually
    serving.

  Where the NestJS concepts live today: config/ is your AppModule plus main.ts.
  The health "controller" is a function view in config/views.py, and its "DTO"
  is HealthSerializer in the same file. The full mapping table is in CLAUDE.md.

⏺ I'm on feat/api-claude-setup. I checked fhir.resources 8.3.0: by default it
  implements FHIR R5, and R4B and STU3 are available as subpackages. Nothing
  written yet; here's the plan.

  Plan

  Files
  - api/AGENTS.md: the backend reference, about 150 lines max.
  - api/CLAUDE.md: one line, @AGENTS.md.
  - Root CLAUDE.md: I'll remove the backend conventions so nothing is
    duplicated, and add a pointer to api/AGENTS.md. Claude Code loads
    api/CLAUDE.md automatically when working in api/.
  - api/pyproject.toml + uv.lock: add fhir.resources.

  Architecture in api/AGENTS.md: one Django app per domain (patients/), one file
  per responsibility.

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  You'd say   │ Django/DRF file │                  Rule                  │
  │   (NestJS)   │                 │                                        │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Model /      │ models.py       │ Fields and DB constraints only, no     │
  │ entity       │                 │ business logic                         │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │                 │ Input validation and output shape. No  │
  │ DTO          │ serializers.py  │ DB writes beyond delegating to         │
  │              │                 │ services                               │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │ views.py        │ HTTP only: permissions, call a         │
  │ Controller   │ (ViewSet)       │ selector or service, return a          │
  │              │                 │ serializer. No ORM queries             │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │                 │ Writes and business rules, as plain    │
  │ Service      │ services.py     │ functions. Owns transactions and audit │
  │              │                 │  logging                               │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ DAO /        │ selectors.py    │ Reads. Every queryset is scoped to     │
  │ repository   │                 │ what the requesting user may access    │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ FHIR mapping │ fhir.py         │ Model ↔ FHIR resource conversion,      │
  │              │                 │ validated with fhir.resources          │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Routing      │ urls.py         │ DRF router                             │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  services.py/selectors.py is a widely used Django convention (HackSoft
  styleguide). In NestJS terms, selectors play the role of your DAO. They aren't
  a repository wrapping the ORM: they use the ORM directly.

  Security and privacy rules (the essentials, each enforceable):
  - IDs: UUID primary keys (Python 3.14 uuid.uuid7). No sequential IDs that can
    be enumerated. The UUID is also the FHIR Patient.id.
  - Access: deny by default. Every patient endpoint needs authentication plus
    object-level authorization, enforced in selectors (filtered querysets). A
    forbidden object returns 404, not 403, so you can't tell it exists.
  - Logging: never log personal health information (PHI): no names, birth dates,
    identifiers or request/response bodies. Log only UUIDs, the user ID, the
    action and the status. Exceptions must not include PHI either.
  - URLs: no PHI in paths or query strings, only UUIDs. Search is client-side,
    so the API needs no filter parameters.
  - Audit trail: every create, read, update and delete on patient data records
    who, what (UUID) and when. The rule goes in now; implementation comes with
    the CRUD step. It's required by health-data regulations (GDPR art. 9, NEN
    7513 if you're in NL, HIPAA in the US).
  - Data minimization: store only the fields the product needs. Don't add a
    field "just in case".
  - Errors: generic API error messages that never echo PHI back.
  - Tests and fixtures: fake data only, never real patient data.
  - Dependencies: added only via uv (locked), and kept to a minimum.

  FHIR rules
  - The database stores a small flat subset (e.g. name, birth date, gender):
    ordinary columns, not the full spec and not a JSON blob.
  - The API represents a patient as a valid FHIR Patient resource (resourceType:
    "Patient"). fhir.py maps between the model and that resource, and tests
    validate the output with fhir.resources.
  - Extending the subset is a deliberate choice: add a column and its mapping,
    never raw FHIR passthrough.

  Testing rules (TDD)
  - Red → green → refactor. Write the failing test first, run it and see it fail
    for the right reason, then implement.
  - Tests live in patients/tests/test_<layer>.py, written as pytest functions
    against the real Postgres test database.
  - Every endpoint gets tests for: unauthenticated → 401/403, another
    practitioner's object → 404, invalid input → 400, plus the happy path.
  - Every FHIR output is validated by fhir.resources in a test.

  Commands: the relevant make targets.

  Questions

  1. FHIR version: R4 (via fhir.resources.R4B) or R5 (the library default)? I
     recommend R4/R4B. It's what nearly everyone runs in production (EHRs,
     national profiles such as the Dutch nl-core and US Core), while R5 adoption
     is still low. For the Patient resource, R4 and R4B are identical.
  2. Root CLAUDE.md: OK to trim its backend section down to a pointer to
     api/AGENTS.md?

  I don't need extra documentation. I know fhir.resources and the FHIR Patient
  resource well enough, and I'll check details against the installed package.

  Architecture, with Django names

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       File        │             Plays the role of (NestJS)              │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ models.py         │ Model / entity                                      │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ serializers.py    │ DTO + validation                                    │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ views.py          │ Controller                                          │
  │ (ViewSets)        │                                                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ services.py       │ Service: write functions, owning transactions and   │
  │                   │ the audit trail                                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ selectors.py      │ DAO: read functions, always filtered to what the    │
  │                   │ user may access                                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ fhir.py           │ Conversion between the model and the FHIR resource  │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Security and privacy rules
  - IDs: UUIDv7 primary keys, never sequential. The UUID is also the FHIR
    Patient.id.
  - Access: deny by default. Each user only gets the records they may access,
    filtered in selectors. Records outside that scope return 404, not 403.
  - Health data: none in logs, URLs or error messages.
  - Audit trail: every create, read, update and delete on patient data is
    recorded.
  - Data minimization: store only what the product needs.
  - Tests: fake data only.
  - Admin: Django admin stays disabled.

  One finding that became a rule. I tested fhir.resources.R4B directly. It
  rejects bad types, bad formats and unknown fields, but it accepted gender: 
  "nope": it doesn't check coded values, even mandatory ones. So AGENTS.md
  requires coded fields to be restricted by our own Django choices
  (TextChoices), copied exactly from the FHIR value sets. Don't rely on the
  library for that.

  Rules that will need code later. The audit trail and the access filtering are
  rules for now, and nothing enforces them yet. They get built in the CRUD and
  auth steps.

  Shall I commit this?

⏺ You're already on feat/data-model, and main includes the merged setup PR.
  Nothing written yet; here's the plan.

  Two Django apps

  ┌───────────┬───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    App    │       Model       │              Why separate               │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ User: the         │ The login identity. Django requires a   │
  │ accounts/ │ practitioner who  │ custom user model to be set before the  │
  │           │ logs in           │ first migration; switching later is     │
  │           │                   │ painful.                                │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ patients/ │ Patient           │ The health data                         │
  └───────────┴───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Trade-off: accounts.User vs practitioners.Practitioner. For now every account
  is a practitioner. I'd still name it User (the Django convention; it's
  settings.AUTH_USER_MODEL) and call it "practitioner" in the domain, through
  Patient.practitioners. If the organization later needs non-practitioner
  accounts (e.g. an admin), nothing has to be renamed.

  accounts.User (the practitioner)

  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Field     │        Type         │               Notes               │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ id           │ UUID (v7), PK       │ Per AGENTS.md                     │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │                     │ Login. Normalized to lowercase,   │
  │ email        │ EmailField, unique  │ plus a DB constraint on           │
  │              │ ignoring case       │ Lower(email) so Jane@x and jane@x │
  │              │                     │  can't both exist                 │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │                     │ Hashed with Argon2 (OWASP         │
  │ password     │ (from               │ recommendation, needs             │
  │              │ AbstractBaseUser)   │ argon2-cffi), never stored in     │
  │              │                     │ plain text                        │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ first_name,  │ CharField           │ Shown to colleagues               │
  │ last_name    │                     │                                   │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │              │                     │ Deactivate instead of deleting,   │
  │ is_active    │ BooleanField        │ so audit entries keep pointing to │
  │              │                     │  a real user                      │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ last_login   │ (from               │                                   │
  │              │ AbstractBaseUser)   │                                   │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ created_at   │ DateTimeField       │                                   │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  - No username, is_staff, groups or permissions. There's no admin, and
    authorization will be "a practitioner sees their own patients". That's why I
    build on AbstractBaseUser, not AbstractUser: a smaller attack surface.
    Roles can be added later if needed.
  - No public signup. A UserManager.create_user(email, password, …) exists for
    code. A management command for the organization to create accounts comes in
    the auth step.
  - In settings: AUTH_USER_MODEL, Argon2 hashers, and password validators
    (minimum 12 characters, no common passwords).

  patients.Patient and FHIR mapping (R4)

  Field: id
  Type: UUID (v7), PK
  FHIR Patient: id
  Notes:
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: family_name
  Type: CharField(150), required
  FHIR Patient: name[0].family
  Notes: name[0].use = "official"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: given_name
  Type: CharField(150), required
  FHIR Patient: name[0].given = [given_name]
  Notes: See trade-off ①
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: birth_date
  Type: DateField, required
  FHIR Patient: birthDate
  Notes: Past dates only (validated in the serializer)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: gender
  Type: TextChoices: male / female / other / unknown
  FHIR Patient: gender
  Notes: Mirrors the FHIR AdministrativeGender value set exactly, with a DB
    CheckConstraint. See trade-off ③
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: phone
  Type: CharField(32), optional
  FHIR Patient: telecom[] {system: "phone", value}
  Notes:
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: email
  Type: EmailField, optional
  FHIR Patient: telecom[] {system: "email", value}
  Notes: Not unique (family members can share one)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: active
  Type: BooleanField, default True
  FHIR Patient: active
  Notes: Archive instead of delete. See trade-off ④
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: created_at
  Type: DateTimeField
  FHIR Patient: (none)
  Notes:
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: updated_at
  Type: DateTimeField
  FHIR Patient: meta.lastUpdated
  Notes:
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Field: practitioners
  Type: ManyToManyField(User, related_name="patients")
  FHIR Patient: not mapped
  Notes: See trade-off ②

  fhir.py (model ↔ FHIR) comes with the CRUD step, since this step is models
  only. The mapping above is what it will implement.

  Trade-offs

  1. given_name as one string, not a list. FHIR's given is a list (["Jean", 
     "Pierre"]). One string is simpler for forms and client-side search, and
     maps to given: ["Jean Pierre"], which is valid FHIR but less precise. A
     Postgres ArrayField would map exactly, at the cost of harder forms.
     Recommendation: one string.
  2. Practitioners aren't part of the FHIR Patient. The closest field,
     generalPractitioner, means the patient's nominated primary care provider
     (their GP), not "the practitioners who follow this patient". Mapping to it
     would be semantically wrong. The relation is an access-control fact. If it
     ever needs to be exposed in FHIR, the right resource is CareTeam. I'll keep
     Django's implicit M2M table (no custom through model). Who assigned whom
     and when will be covered by the audit trail, not by extra columns.
  3. Not stored, on purpose (data minimization):
     - National ID (BSN, NIR, SSN): the most sensitive identifier, with strict
       legal limits on processing. Add it only when a real workflow needs it,
       e.g. exchanging data with another system.
     - Address: more personal data, a complex FHIR structure, and not needed for
       a basic record.
     - Emergency contact and marital status.

     Each can be added later as a deliberate choice, per AGENTS.md.
  4. active instead of deletion. Medical records have legal retention periods
     (e.g. 20 years in NL under the WGBO), and audit entries must keep pointing
     to something. Hard deletion is a separate decision for the CRUD step.
  5. No field-level encryption. It would break DB constraints and client-side
     search, and adds key management. Protection comes from encrypted disks and
     backups at the infrastructure level, TLS for DB connections, and access
     control. I'll add this to the README trade-offs.
  6. No extra indexes. Search is client side. Primary keys, the unique email and
     the M2M table already have indexes.

  Implementation (TDD)

  1. Write failing tests in accounts/tests/test_models.py and
     patients/tests/test_models.py:
     - email normalized and unique ignoring case
     - passwords hashed with Argon2
     - inactive users can't authenticate
     - UUID primary keys
     - an invalid gender is rejected by the DB
     - the M2M relation works both ways
  2. Run them and watch them fail, then implement the models, settings and
     migrations.
  3. Add a project-level test that fails if a model change has no migration
     (makemigrations --check).
  4. Dev database reset needed (make reset). The default auth_user table was
     created by the first migration. Switching to a custom user model needs a
     fresh DB, and there's no data to lose.
  5. Since there are no new endpoints, openapi.yaml stays unchanged.

  Questions

  1. Do you agree to store patient phone and email (optional), or should the
     record be name + birth date + gender only?
  2. OK with one given_name string (trade-off ①)?

❯ Looks good, I agree with the single User table. We keep phone and email, a    
  practitioner should be able to contact the patient. Ok with one given_name.   
  And why uuid v7 instead of v4 ?                                               

⏺ Short answer: v7 is faster for the database, v4 reveals nothing. Thinking it
  through for this project, I'd switch to v4.

  ┌────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │                │           UUID v4           │         UUID v7          │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Content        │ 122 random bits             │ 48-bit timestamp (ms) +  │
  │                │                             │ 74 random bits           │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Ordering       │ Random                      │ Sorted by creation time  │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ DB index       │ Inserts land anywhere in    │ Inserts append at the    │
  │ (B-tree)       │ the index, so pages split   │ end, like an             │
  │                │ and the index fragments     │ auto-increment           │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Guessing /     │ Impossible                  │ Still impossible (74     │
  │ enumeration    │                             │ random bits)             │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Leaks          │                             │ The creation time of the │
  │ information    │ Nothing                     │  record, to the          │
  │                │                             │ millisecond              │
  └────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  Why I first proposed v7: it's the modern default. It's ordered like a sequence
  without the enumeration risk, and Python 3.14 now generates it natively.

  Why v4 fits this app better:
  - v7's only real benefit is insert performance, and it only matters at
    millions of rows with heavy write traffic. A patient app for a few
    practitioners will have thousands of rows. The difference is unmeasurable.
  - v7 puts data inside the ID. The patient UUID appears in URLs, logs, the
    audit trail and the FHIR Patient.id. With v7, anyone who sees the ID can
    read when the record was created, i.e. roughly when this person became a
    patient. That's a small leak, but it's health-related metadata, and our
    rules say IDs should carry no information.
  - Ordering isn't needed: sort by created_at explicitly when needed.

  So for health data at this scale I'd use v4 (default=uuid.uuid4) for all
  models and update the rule in AGENTS.md.

  Shall I switch to v4 and start the TDD implementation, or keep v7?

      90  - **The Compose stack is a development setup** (Vite dev server
          , Django `runserver`, bind mounts).
      91    A production deployment would build the frontend (`npm run bu
          ild`), serve it and the API behind a
      92    TLS reverse proxy (set `DJANGO_BEHIND_TLS_PROXY=true`), and u
          se the api image's default gunicorn command.
      93 +- **No field-level encryption of patient data.** It would break
         + database constraints and                                      
      94 +  client-side search, and requires key management. Data at rest
         + is protected at the infrastructure                            
      95 +  level (encrypted disks and backups), in transit by TLS (`POST
         +GRES_SSLMODE`), and in the app by                              
      96 +  access control.

⏺ The data model is done on feat/data-model, written test-first. Not committed
  yet.

  Results
  - Tests: all 34 pass (25 new). Before any model code existed, the run failed
    because User couldn't be imported, and the migration check failed on the
    missing migrations.
  - Checks: lint passes on both sides. make check-deploy shows only the expected
    HSTS preload warning.
  - API: openapi.yaml is unchanged, since there are no new endpoints.
  - Generated SQL: I read the migration's SQL. The gender value set, the
    non-empty name checks and the lowercase-email rule are real Postgres CHECK
    constraints, not just Django validation.

  What was added
  - accounts.User (api/accounts/models.py) is the practitioner who logs in.
    - Random UUID primary key (v4).
    - Login by email, case-insensitive: emails are stored lowercase (enforced by
      the DB) and unique, and login lowercases what the user types.
    - No username, staff flag or permissions.
    - is_active lets you deactivate an account instead of deleting it.
    - create_user() is the only way to create an account; there's no signup.
  - patients.Patient (api/patients/models.py) has exactly the fields we agreed
    on, with the FHIR mapping written in its docstring.
    - Gender mirrors the FHIR value set.
    - The many-to-many relation practitioners ↔ user.patients covers patients
      followed by several practitioners and vice versa.
    - str() returns only the ID on both models, so names never end up in logs.
  - Settings:
    - AUTH_USER_MODEL points to the new accounts.User.
    - Passwords are hashed with Argon2 only (argon2-cffi added).
    - Password rules: at least 12 characters, not a common password, not all
      digits, not too close to the user's own details.
  - Tests: shared fixtures make_user / make_patient live in api/conftest.py. A
    project-level test fails if a model change has no migration.

  Details worth knowing
  - You must run make reset once. Switching to the custom user model needs a
    fresh dev database. I did it on mine; you'll need to do it on yours too (it
    deletes the dev data, which is empty for now).
  - Gender is at module level (from patients.models import Gender), not nested
    inside Patient. The nested version broke the DB constraint: Django's Meta
    can't see classes nested in the model.
  - Ruff config changes:
    - Ruff now ignores Django migrations (they're generated).
    - It allows assert and fake passwords in tests.
    - It ignores RUF012, which flags the plain class-level lists Django uses
      (Meta.constraints, REQUIRED_FIELDS).
  - Docs: AGENTS.md now says UUIDv4, not v7, and points to the shared fixtures.
    The README has the field-encryption trade-off.

  Left for later steps
  - A patient with no practitioner would be visible to nobody. A database can't
    require at least one entry in a many-to-many relation, so the create service
    will assign the practitioner who creates the patient (CRUD step).
  - The account-creation command for the organization comes in the auth step.

  Shall I commit and push?

⏺ You're on feat/auth, and main includes the merged data-model PR. Nothing
  written yet; here's the plan.

  Auth mechanism: Django sessions (HttpOnly cookie) + CSRF

  Where the browser keeps it
  Session cookie (recommended): HttpOnly cookie: JavaScript can't read it, so
    an XSS can't steal it
  DRF Token: localStorage: readable by any XSS
  JWT: localStorage or a cookie
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Logout / revocation
  Session cookie (recommended): Immediate (session deleted server-side)
  DRF Token: Delete the token
  JWT: Hard: stays valid until it expires (needs a denylist)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Deactivated account
  Session cookie (recommended): Rejected on its next request
  DRF Token: Needs extra code
  JWT: Valid until expiry
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Idle timeout
  Session cookie (recommended): Built in
  DRF Token: Not built in
  JWT: Needs refresh-token logic
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  CSRF to handle
  Session cookie (recommended): Yes
  DRF Token: No
  JWT: Depends
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Extra dependencies
  Session cookie (recommended): None (built into Django)
  DRF Token: None
  JWT: djangorestframework-simplejwt

  The frontend and API share one origin through the proxy, which is exactly the
  case sessions are designed for. JWT only pays off for third-party clients or
  many separate services, and for health data the revocation weakness is a real
  problem. The cost of sessions is CSRF. Django handles it; the frontend just
  sends an X-CSRFToken header on POST requests, wired once in the Orval fetch
  wrapper later.

  Endpoints

  Route: GET /api/auth/csrf/
  Access: Public
  Behaviour: Sets the csrftoken cookie (the SPA calls it before logging in).
    Returns 204
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Route: POST /api/auth/login/
  Access: Public, CSRF-protected, rate-limited
  Behaviour: {email, password} → 200 with the user. Starts a new session
    (Django rotates the session ID, blocking session fixation)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Route: POST /api/auth/logout/
  Access: Authenticated, CSRF
  Behaviour: Deletes the session → 204. POST, not GET, so a link or image
    can't log someone out
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Route: GET /api/auth/me/
  Access: Authenticated
  Behaviour: {id, email, first_name, last_name}

  Behaviour
  - Not logged in → 401 everywhere (DRF's default here is 403). The frontend can
    then tell "please log in" (401) from "forbidden" (403).
  - Wrong credentials → 400 with one generic message, whether the email is
    unknown, the password is wrong or the account is deactivated. Nobody can
    find out which emails have accounts. Django also runs a dummy password hash
    for unknown emails, so response times give nothing away.
  - Login CSRF: the login view enforces CSRF even though the user isn't logged
    in yet. Otherwise an attacker could silently log a practitioner into the
    attacker's own account.
  - Rate limiting: 5 login attempts per minute per IP (DRF throttling) → 429.
    Throttle counters must be shared across gunicorn workers, so they're stored
    in Django's database cache table. That's no new service, just one
    createcachetable at startup.

  Session settings (healthcare-grade)

  - Cookie flags: HttpOnly and SameSite=Lax, plus Secure outside DEBUG (already
    set).
  - Idle timeout of 30 minutes, renewed on every request: automatic logoff is a
    standard healthcare requirement. Trade-off: each request also writes to the
    sessions table, which is negligible at our scale.
  - Inactive accounts: a deactivated account's existing session is rejected on
    its next request (built into Django).
  - Password changes: changing a password invalidates the account's other
    sessions (built into Django).
  - Origin check: the Vite proxy will keep the original Host header
    (changeOrigin: false), so Django's CSRF origin check passes without listing
    trusted origins. The production reverse proxy must do the same.

  Management command

  make create-practitioner runs python manage.py create_practitioner --email … 
  --first-name … --last-name ….
  - The password is prompted twice, never passed as an argument. Arguments end
    up in shell history and the process list.
  - Validation: the password must pass the password rules (12+ characters, not a
    common password…). The email is normalized, and a duplicate (in any case)
    gives a clean error, not a stack trace.
  - Output: it prints only the new account's UUID.

  Code layout (per AGENTS.md)

  accounts/serializers.py (LoginSerializer, UserSerializer), accounts/views.py,
  accounts/urls.py, accounts/management/commands/create_practitioner.py.
  - No services.py or selectors.py: login and logout are Django's own
    authenticate/login/logout, so a services file would be empty. Per AGENTS.md,
    no empty layers.
  - Logs: login success and logout are logged with the user UUID, and failures
    without the email (it's personal data). The proper audit table comes with
    patient CRUD.

  Tests first (accounts/tests/test_views.py, test_commands.py)

  - CSRF: the csrf endpoint sets the cookie. Login and logout without a CSRF
    token → 403 (tested with a client that enforces CSRF).
  - Login:
    - Success: 200, user returned, sessionid cookie set with HttpOnly, and the
      session ID changes.
    - Unknown email, wrong password and inactive account all return the same 400
      response.
    - The 6th attempt within a minute → 429.
  - /me: 401 when anonymous, 200 when logged in. The response never contains the
    password hash.
  - Logout: 204, then /me → 401.
  - Deactivated account: a practitioner deactivated while logged in gets 401 on
    their next request.
  - Logs: no email or password in the logs on failed login (checked with
    pytest's caplog).
  - Session settings: a test pins the 30-minute timeout and the cookie flags.
  - Command:
    - Creates the account with an Argon2-hashed password.
    - Rejects mismatched passwords, weak passwords and duplicate emails.
    - Has no --password option.
  - PUBLIC_ENDPOINTS gets csrf and login. Then make schema.

  Questions

  1. Is a 30-minute idle timeout OK? 15 minutes is stricter and common in
     hospitals, but annoying for practitioners. 30 minutes is a usual
     compromise.
  2. Is 5 login attempts per minute per IP OK, or do you also want account 
     lockout after N failures? Lockout stops slow, distributed attacks on one
     account, but it lets anyone lock a practitioner out. I'd skip it for now.

✻ Worked for 1m 1s · done Friday 8:52 PM

  production 2FA is better than a lockout + organization security policy should 
  decide for the idle timeout

⏺ Auth and the seeded demo accounts are done on feat/auth, built test-first. Not
  committed yet.

  Results
  - Tests: 67 pass, 32 of them new. The view and security tests failed first (21
    failures); the command tests couldn't load until the command existed.
  - Checks: lint passes on both sides, typecheck too, and check-deploy shows
    only the expected HSTS preload warning.
  - Manual check: I ran the whole flow with curl through the Vite proxy, sending
    the Origin header like a browser. Login without a CSRF token returns 403,
    login with it returns 200 and sets an HttpOnly sessionid cookie, and after
    logout /me returns 401.
  - Seeding: make up creates Alice and Bob. On restart, the logs show "already
    exists, skipped".

  Endpoints (/api/auth/)

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │                         Route                          │ Generated hook  │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ GET csrf/: public, sets the CSRF cookie                │ useGetCsrfToken │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ POST login/: public, CSRF-protected, 5 attempts/min    │ useLogin        │
  │ per IP                                                 │                 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ POST logout/                                           │ useLogout       │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ GET me/                                                │ useGetMe        │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  Fixes you might not expect
  - Rate limit bypass: by default DRF identifies the client by trusting the
    X-Forwarded-For header, which anyone can forge. That would have made the
    login rate limit trivial to bypass. I set NUM_PROXIES to 0 by default and 1
    behind the TLS proxy, and a test covers it.
  - Login CSRF: DRF skips CSRF checks for anonymous requests, so the login view
    enforces it explicitly.
  - Public-endpoint test: it only looked at top-level URLs, so it would have
    missed everything under api/auth/. It now walks all routes.
  - Vite proxy: I changed changeOrigin to false so Django's CSRF origin check
    passes without a trusted-origins list.

  Management command: make create-practitioner
  - Interactive by default: it asks for the details and prompts for the password
    twice. It refuses weak passwords, invalid emails and duplicates (in any
    case), and prints only the new UUID.
  - New options added for seeding:
    - --no-input reads the password from $PRACTITIONER_PASSWORD, the same
      convention as Django's createsuperuser.
    - --skip-existing makes it safe to run on every start.
  - The password can never be passed as an argument; a test checks it.

  Seeding: done in the compose api command, not in the Docker image, so
  production never creates these accounts. The README lists the credentials:
  alice@example.com / alice-local-dev-only and bob@example.com /
  bob-local-dev-only.

  Docs
  - README has an auth section and the trade-offs you asked for:
    - demo accounts are local dev only
    - two-factor authentication is better than lockout in production
    - the idle timeout is a default the organization's security policy should
      set (now configurable with DJANGO_SESSION_IDLE_TIMEOUT)
    - rate-limit counters live in the DB cache, so production needs
      createcachetable once, like migrations
  - api/AGENTS.md has a short Authentication section.
  - Root CLAUDE.md explains how the frontend sends the CSRF token.

  Shall I commit and push?

※ recap: We're building a secure, FHIR-ready patient management app, and session auth with seeded demo practitioners is now committed and pushed on feat/auth. Next is planning the patient CRUD endpoints, with fake patients seeded per practitioner to show scoping.

❯ patient CRUD as a REST API, exposed as FHIR R4B Patient resources.A           
  practitioner only sees and edits their own patients; creating a patient links 
  it to the creator. List returns all their active patients (search is          
  client-side, no server filters or pagination write it as a tradeoff). Delete  
  archives the patient (active = false). Seed a few obviously fake patients per 
  demo practitioner, so reviewers can see the scoping. Update the OpenAPI       
  schema and the generated frontend client. Be extremely careful the error      
  codes and error messages. Show me your plan first (be more concise in your    
  plan message).                                                                

⏺ Plan: patient CRUD (FHIR R4)

  Endpoints (PatientViewSet + DRF router, /api/patients/)

  ┌────────┬────────┬────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Method │ Route  │  Success   │                Behaviour                 │
  ├────────┼────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │        │        │            │ FHIR Bundle (type: searchset) of the     │
  │ GET    │ /      │ 200        │ practitioner's active patients, sorted   │
  │        │        │            │ by name                                  │
  ├────────┼────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ POST   │ /      │ 201 +      │ Creates the patient and links it to the  │
  │        │        │ Location   │ creator                                  │
  ├────────┼────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GET    │ /{id}/ │ 200        │ FHIR Patient                             │
  ├────────┼────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PUT    │ /{id}/ │ 200        │ Full replacement, the FHIR way. A body   │
  │        │        │            │ id that differs from the URL → 400       │
  ├────────┼────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │        │        │            │ Archives the patient (active=false).     │
  │ DELETE │ /{id}/ │ 204        │ From then on it's treated as deleted →   │
  │        │        │            │ 404                                      │
  └────────┴────────┴────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  No PATCH. Responses are application/fhir+json (and application/json if the
  client asks for it), and both content types are accepted as input.

  Errors: FHIR OperationOutcome on patient endpoints, the FHIR-standard error
  format: {resourceType, issue: [{severity, code, diagnostics, expression}]}.

  Status: 400
  Issue code: structure / required / value / not-supported
  When: Malformed JSON or wrong types / missing element / invalid value / FHIR
    element we don't support (e.g. address). expression points at the element
    (Patient.birthDate)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Status: 401
  Issue code: login
  When: Not logged in
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Status: 403
  Issue code: forbidden
  When: Missing CSRF token
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Status: 404
  Issue code: not-found
  When: One identical message (Patient not found.) for: doesn't exist, belongs
    to another practitioner, archived, malformed UUID. Nothing reveals that a
    patient exists
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Status: 405 / 406 / 415
  Issue code: not-supported
  When: Wrong method / Accept header / Content-Type

  Error messages never echo submitted values. fhir.resources (pydantic) errors
  include the raw input, so I map them to the element path plus a fixed message
  and never pass the original text through.

  Input rules
  - Required: exactly one name with family and one given, gender (one of the
    FHIR codes), birthDate. The birth date must be a full date (FHIR also allows
    1980 or 1980-05) and not in the future.
  - telecom: at most one phone and one email (valid format).
  - Server-managed elements (id, meta, active) are accepted and ignored, so a
    client can GET then PUT the same resource. The exception is an id mismatch,
    which returns 400.

  Code (AGENTS layers):
  - patients/fhir.py: model → R4B Patient and FHIR JSON → model fields.
  - serializers.py: describes the FHIR shape for OpenAPI/Orval and delegates to
    fhir.py.
  - selectors.py: patient_list and patient_get, always filtered by practitioner.
  - services.py: patient_create, patient_update, patient_archive.
  - views.py + urls.py.

  Seed (local dev only): a seed_demo_patients command, idempotent, run by
  compose.
  - Alice: Alpha Demo, Bravo Demo, Charlie Demo. Bob: Delta Demo, Echo Demo.
  - Shared Demo is followed by both, to show the many-to-many relation.
  - All with @example.com emails and phone numbers that obviously aren't real.

  Tests first:
  - FHIR output validated by fhir.resources, plus a round trip (GET → PUT).
  - Every 400 case, with no submitted value appearing in the response.
  - Scoping: GET/PUT/DELETE on another practitioner's patient → 404, and the
    list excludes other practitioners' and archived patients.
  - 401/403/405/415.
  - Location header and content type.
  - The seed command creates nothing on a second run.

  Then make schema, which regenerates the frontend hooks.

  Trade-offs (README)
  - No pagination or server-side filters (search is client side): fine for a
    practitioner's caseload of hundreds of patients, not for tens of thousands.
  - No optimistic locking (ETag / If-Match): if two practitioners edit the same
    patient at once, the last write wins.
  - Archived patients return 404 instead of FHIR's 410 Gone, and there's no
    restore endpoint.
  - No PATCH.
  - One name, one phone and one email per patient.

  Question: the audit trail. AGENTS.md says every create, read, update and
  delete on patient data must be recorded. Do I build it in this PR (a small
  PatientAuditEvent model written by services and selectors: who, which patient,
  which action, when), or in a dedicated PR right after? I'd do it right after,
  to keep this PR reviewable. The rule stays, and the next PR enforces it.

⏺ Patient CRUD is done on feat/crud-patient, built test-first. Not committed
  yet.

  Results
  - Tests: 133 backend tests pass, 66 of them new. They all failed before the
    code existed (FHIR module missing, routes missing, seed command unknown).
  - Checks: lint passes on both sides, typecheck too, and check-deploy shows
    only the usual HSTS preload warning.
  - Manual check: I ran Alice and Bob through the Vite proxy with curl. Each
    sees only their own patients plus Shared, and Bob gets 404 on Alice's new
    patient. An invalid PUT returns a 400 OperationOutcome listing both problems
    without echoing any value, and a deleted patient returns 404.

  API (/api/patients/)

  ┌──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Generated hook      │                   Endpoint                    │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ useListPatients          │ GET /: FHIR Bundle of active patients the     │
  │                          │ practitioner follows, sorted by name          │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ useCreatePatient         │ POST /: 201 + Location, linked to the creator │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ useGetPatient /          │ GET / PUT /{id}/                              │
  │ useUpdatePatient         │                                               │
  ├──────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ useDeletePatient         │ DELETE /{id}/: stop following, 204            │
  └──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Archiving a shared patient
  - DELETE removes only the requesting practitioner's link, and active = false
    is set only when the last follower leaves. Patients are never hard-deleted.
  - The patient row is locked during that check, so two simultaneous deletes
    can't leave an active patient that nobody follows.
  - The test you asked for is test_deleting_a_shared_patient_only_unfollows_it.
    It's in the README trade-offs.

  Errors
  - All errors are FHIR OperationOutcomes with an issue code and the element
    path, e.g. value on Patient.birthDate.
  - Validation reports all problems in one response.
  - The same 404 (Patient not found.) covers someone else's patient, an archived
    one, an unknown ID and a malformed ID.

  Things you might not expect
  - Raw validation errors are never shown. fhir.resources errors contain
    submitted values, and unknown keys are client text. Messages are fixed
    strings, and expression only ever contains real FHIR element names; tests
    check this.
  - fhir.resources accepts partial birth dates (1980) and system: "fax" (both
    valid FHIR), so the subset rules reject them explicitly.
  - No ?format= parameter. DRF was documenting one, which added noise to the
    generated client. I disabled it, so content negotiation only uses the Accept
    header.

  Seed (local dev only): seed_demo_patients runs after the accounts in compose
  and creates nothing on later runs.

  Docs: the README has the patient section and trade-offs (no pagination or
  filters, shared archive, no optimistic locking, 404 instead of 410, no PATCH,
  one name/phone/email). AGENTS.md has the OperationOutcome and archive rules,
  and CLAUDE.md explains how the frontend should read errors.

  Leftover in your dev database: my end-to-end check left one archived "Demo
  E2E" patient. It's invisible everywhere; make reset removes it.

  Shall I commit and push?

  selectors from that branch. A branch cut from main today wouldn't have them.
  Two options:
  1. Merge the CRUD PR first, then I branch from the updated main. Simplest
     history.
  2. Stack it: I branch feat/audit-trail from feat/crud-patient now, and its PR
     targets feat/crud-patient until that one is merged.

  Meanwhile, here's the audit trail plan.

  Plan: audit trail

  Model: AuditEvent in a small audit/ app, so login events can reuse it later.

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Field         │                      Notes                      │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ id                    │ UUIDv4                                          │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ occurred_at           │ Timestamp, set by the DB                        │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ actor                 │ FK to the user, PROTECT (an audited user can't  │
  │                       │ be deleted)                                     │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ action                │ create / read / update / delete, mirroring FHIR │
  │                       │  AuditEvent's C/R/U/D codes                     │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ resource_type /       │ "Patient" + UUID. No foreign key, so it stays   │
  │ resource_id           │ generic                                         │
  └───────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Rules
  - No personal health data in the audit, only IDs.
  - Append-only, enforced by Postgres. A trigger (in a migration) rejects UPDATE
    and DELETE on the table, so even a code bug or a shell session can't
    rewrite history. Tests check it.
  - Recorded in the same transaction as the change. No write goes through
    without its audit entry, and if the audit insert fails, the request fails
    too.

  What gets recorded, and where

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Action         │                  Recorded by                   │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Create                 │ patient_create                                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Update                 │ patient_update                                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Delete (unfollow or    │ patient_archive                                │
  │ archive)               │                                                │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Read one               │ patient_get                                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ List                   │ patient_list, one read per patient returned    │
  │                        │ (one bulk insert)                              │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Tests first
  - Each endpoint writes exactly the expected events, with the actor and patient
    ID.
  - Requests that fail (404, 400) write nothing.
  - The audit contains no names.
  - UPDATE and DELETE on the table are refused by the DB.
  - A failed audit insert rolls the change back.

  Out of scope (README trade-offs)
  - No API or UI to read the audit yet: it's read via the DB.
  - No retention or purge policy.
  - No IP or client information recorded.
  - Login and logout events aren't recorded yet.

  Which branch option do you want? And OK with the plan?

⏺ Swagger UI at http://localhost:8000/api/docs/ now works with session login and
  CSRF. You can log in as a demo account and call the patient endpoints from
  it. I checked the full flow with curl because the Chrome extension wasn't
  connected; I didn't click through the real page.

  The bug: Swagger UI was already public, but it couldn't make an unsafe call
  after logging in. drf-spectacular puts the CSRF token into the page script
  when the page loads, and Django replaces that token on login. I reproduced it:
  login → 200, then POST /api/patients/ → 403 CSRF token missing or invalid.

  The fix is in api/config/settings.py, in SWAGGER_UI_SETTINGS. Swagger UI now
  reads the csrftoken cookie before each unsafe request and sends it as
  X-CSRFToken, the same way the frontend does. It only does this for requests to
  the API's own origin, so the token never goes anywhere else. I also set
  CSRF_COOKIE_NAME = "csrftoken" explicitly (it's Django's default) so the
  frontend, Swagger and the test all refer to one value.

  Nothing sensitive is exposed: the page and the schema only describe routes and
  request/response shapes, never data. Every patient and /me endpoint still
  requires a login (the PUBLIC_ENDPOINTS test is unchanged).

  Verification
  - A new test in api/tests/test_schema.py checks the Swagger script reads the
    cookie at request time. It failed before the fix and passes now.
  - The curl run copied what the new script does, starting with no cookies:
    loading the page sets the cookie, then login 200, me 200, create 201, list
    200, update 200, delete 204, logout 204, and list after logout 401.
  - 134 backend and 2 frontend tests pass, lint is clean, and check-deploy shows
    only the expected HSTS preload warning.

  README: a new "Try the API manually (Swagger UI)" section gives the URL and
  three steps: log in as Alice, call the patient endpoints, log out and compare
  with Bob. The Swagger trade-off now says how a deployment could hide the docs
  if it wanted to.

  The curl run left an archived "Demo Swagger" patient in your dev database. It
  isn't visible in the API.

  Nothing is committed yet.

✻ Crunched for 26s · done 11:32 AM

› Message from general-purpose
  ## Backend review: findings (most severe first)

  I found three real bugs. All three were reproduced against the running stack
  or the test database. The rest are low-severity items or nits. The
  access-control and auth basics are solid.

  **1. A PUT that overlaps a DELETE brings an archived patient back as active,
  with no followers**
  - Severity: medium · Category: correctness / privacy
  - Location: `api/patients/services.py:20-25` (`patient_update`), together with
  `api/patients/views.py:134-137`
  - What is wrong: `patient_update` does a full `patient.save()` on an object
  the selector loaded earlier, without locking it. That writes every column,
  including the stale `active=True`. The row lock in `patient_archive` only
  orders DELETEs against each other, not against PUTs.
  - Failure scenario: Alice is the patient's only follower. Her PUT loads the
  patient. Her DELETE (another tab, or a retry) commits: link removed,
  `active=False`. The PUT then saves `active=True`. The patient is now active
  and followed by nobody: unreachable by anyone, never archived. This is exactly
  the state the README says the lock prevents. It also lets a practitioner
  write to a record they stopped following during the request.
  - Verified: a scratch pytest against the test DB (file in the container's
  `/tmp`, deleted afterwards) ran this exact interleaving with the real selector
  and services. Result: `active=True`, 0 followers. Confirmed.
  - Fix: in `patient_update`, re-fetch with `select_for_update()` scoped to the
  user (404 if gone) and save with `update_fields=[*data, "updated_at"]`.

  **2. A NUL character in a name gives a 500 instead of a 400 OperationOutcome**
  - Severity: medium · Category: correctness
  - Location: `api/patients/fhir.py:213-219` (`_required_text`), reaching
  `api/patients/services.py:14` / `:24`
  - What is wrong: the FHIR `string` type accepts `\u0000` (its regex `\S`
  matches it). PostgreSQL text columns reject it, so psycopg raises `DataError`.
  The exception handler doesn't catch it, so Django returns an HTML 500. Email
  (`validate_email`) and phone (the regex) are already safe; `family` and
  `given` are not.
  - Failure scenario: `POST /api/patients/` with
  `"name":[{"family":"Demo\u0000x","given":["Nul"]}]` returns 500 (HTML, not
  `application/fhir+json`). PUT goes through the same path. No row is written
  (atomic rollback). The log holds only the psycopg message, no values.
  - Verified: curl, 500 confirmed. The traceback in `docker compose logs api`
  shows `DataError: PostgreSQL text fields cannot contain NUL (0x00) bytes`.
  - Fix: in `_required_text`, reject values containing `"\x00"` (or any control
  character) with an `Issue("value", "Invalid value.", expression)`, and add a
  test.

  **3. Deeply nested JSON gives a 500 (RecursionError), anonymously on login**
  - Severity: low · Category: correctness / security (DoS-ish)
  - Location: `api/accounts/views.py:48` (`request.data`) and
  `api/patients/views.py:109,135`. The default DRF `JSONParser` only turns
  `ValueError` into a ParseError.
  - What is wrong: `json.loads` (or pydantic, for nested `meta.extension`)
  raises `RecursionError`, which isn't handled.
  - Failure scenario: any client gets a CSRF token from the public
  `/api/auth/csrf/`, then POSTs `[[[…]]]` (100k levels, about 200 KB) to
  `/api/auth/login/`. Result: 500 with a full traceback logged. The login
  throttle (5/min per IP) caps the rate. On patient endpoints the same happens
  to authenticated users, including through nested `meta.extension`. With
  `DEBUG` off nothing leaks; it is just a 500 instead of a 400 and noisy logs.
  - Verified: curl. Anonymous login returned 500, and both payloads on
  `/api/patients/` returned 500 `RecursionError`. Confirmed.
  - Fix: subclass the parser and catch `RecursionError` as `ParseError` (used as
  the patient parsers and set as the default in settings), and cap the body
  size with `DATA_UPLOAD_MAX_MEMORY_SIZE`.

  **4. Unknown or unhandled exceptions on FHIR endpoints aren't
  OperationOutcomes**
  - Severity: low · Category: agents-md / correctness
  - Location: `api/patients/views.py:59-61`
  - What is wrong: when DRF returns `None`, Django's HTML 500 page is served.
  This breaks the contract that FHIR endpoints always return OperationOutcomes
  (findings 2 and 3 show it). Nothing leaks with `DEBUG` off.
  - Verified: read the code, plus the curl results above. Confirmed.
  - Fix: return a generic 500 `OperationOutcome` (`code: "exception"`, fixed
  message) after logging the exception.

  **5. `meta` content is silently dropped**
  - Severity: low · Category: agents-md
  - Location: `api/patients/fhir.py:31-36`, `:113-117`
  - What is wrong: AGENTS.md says unsupported input is rejected with a 400,
  never silently dropped. But `meta.tag`, `meta.security`, `meta.extension` and
  `meta.profile` are validated and then thrown away. A client can set a security
  label (for example "restricted") and get a 201 with the label gone, which
  matters for health data.
  - Verified: curl. A POST with `meta.extension` + `meta.tag` returned 201, and
  the response `meta` contained only `lastUpdated`. Confirmed.
  - Fix: accept only `meta.lastUpdated` / `meta.versionId` and report any other
  `meta.*` key as `not-supported`.

  **6. The login throttle is check-then-write, so parallel requests get past
  it**
  - Severity: low · Category: security
  - Location: `api/accounts/views.py:20-26`, `api/config/settings.py:178`
  - What is wrong: DRF's `SimpleRateThrottle` reads the history, then writes it,
  without atomicity (database cache). N parallel attempts can all see fewer
  than 5 and pass. With gunicorn's 3 sync workers this is roughly 3× the limit,
  not unlimited. The README's "5 attempts per minute" is therefore approximate.
  - Verified: read only. Plausible (known DRF behaviour).
  - Fix: accept it and document it, or use an atomic counter (`cache.add` +
  `cache.incr`) for the login scope.

  **7. Tests are missing for the security and concurrency properties the README
  claims**
  - Severity: low · Category: tests
  - Location: `api/patients/tests/` (there is no `test_services.py` /
  `test_selectors.py`, although AGENTS.md says test files mirror the source
  files)
  - What is wrong:
    - Nothing tests the concurrent archive lock or archive vs. update (finding
  1).
    - No test checks that the selector scoping excludes archived or other
  practitioners' patients at function level.
    - No test checks that a 404 PUT/DELETE on someone else's patient leaves the
  DB unchanged. `test_inaccessible_patients_are_indistinguishable` only checks
  the response.
    - No test covers NUL or deep-JSON input.
  - Verified: read only.
  - Fix: add `test_services.py` with an interleaving test like the one in
  finding 1, plus DB-unchanged assertions in the cross-user test.

  **8. Nits**
  - `api/patients/fhir.py:242` uses `datetime.date.today()`.
  `django.utils.timezone.localdate()` is the idiomatic choice. No impact today
  (TZ is UTC everywhere).
  - `api/patients/fhir.py:154` compares the body `id` as a string: an uppercase
  UUID that is otherwise valid gets rejected as a mismatch. Compare parsed
  UUIDs.
  - `api/patients/management/commands/seed_demo_patients.py:34` calls
  `User.objects.get_by_natural_key` outside the files where ORM calls are
  allowed (`selectors` / `services` / `models`), against the AGENTS.md rule.
  `create_practitioner.py:59` does the same with `create_user`.
  - `api/Dockerfile:21,24`: `COPY --chown=app:app` lets the runtime user rewrite
  the app's own code. Keeping code root-owned (only `/opt/venv` readable) is
  stronger hardening.
  - `api/Dockerfile:1`: the base image is not pinned by digest.
  - `.dockerignore` does not exclude `.env*` or `tests/`.

  ### Data I created on the dev database
  - One patient, family "Demo", given "T" (id
  `18ee57a6-f602-4b4a-a060-6d47c66f2147`), created by alice while testing `meta`
  (finding 5). I then DELETEd it, so it is now archived (`active=false`); the
  row remains, as designed.
  - No row was written by the 500s (the transaction rolled back).
  - A few failed logins went to `/api/auth/login/` (they count toward the
  throttle).
  - The race test (finding 1) ran only on the pytest test database.

  ### Checked and found OK
  - Deny by default: `IsAuthenticated` is the default, the 5 public endpoints
  match `PUBLIC_ENDPOINTS`, and a test enforces it.
  - Object scoping is in the selector (`practitioners=user, active=True`).
  Another practitioner's, archived, unknown and malformed ids all return the
  same 404 on GET, PUT and DELETE.
  - 401 vs 403: the `WWW-Authenticate: Session` header is set. A deactivated
  user loses access immediately (DRF checks `is_active`).
  - CSRF is enforced on login (anonymous), logout, and patient POST/PUT/DELETE.
  Login rotates the session and the CSRF token.
  - No user enumeration: one generic error; ModelBackend hashes on an unknown
  email; an inactive account takes the same path.
  - `NUM_PROXIES=0` ignores `X-Forwarded-For`; behind the proxy, exactly 1 hop
  is trusted, matching `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER`.
  - PHI: the `__str__` methods are UUID-only, logs contain only user ids,
  OperationOutcome diagnostics are fixed strings, and expressions carry only
  element names (unknown keys are masked). There are no query parameters or
  search.
  - Production settings: `DEBUG` defaults to off, the secret key and DB settings
  are required, `ALLOWED_HOSTS` fails closed, cookies are secure and HSTS is on
  when not in DEBUG. `check --deploy` shows only the HSTS-preload warning.
  - Headers: CSP `default-src 'none'`, the Swagger UI CSP override, XFO DENY,
  nosniff, Referrer-Policy. JSON only (406 for HTML).
  - FHIR input edge cases return a proper 400:
    - `name:[null]`, `telecom:[null]`, `given:[null]`
    - integer values for `birthDate`, `family` and the telecom value
    - Feb 30, year 0000, Arabic-Indic digits, a trailing `\n` in `birthDate`
    - `gender` as a list, `_family`, a NUL in phone or email
    - invalid nested `meta` or `period`
  - Shared patients: DELETE only unfollows, and the patient is archived when the
  last follower leaves (DELETE vs DELETE is serialized by `select_for_update`).
  No hard delete.
  - Migrations: constraints match the models, and the makemigrations check
  passes.
  - Containers run as non-root, `seed_dev.sh` is excluded from the image, and
  ports are bound to 127.0.0.1.
  - `make test-api`: 134 passed. `ruff check`: clean.

⏺ The fresh reviewer is done. It found three real bugs, each reproduced on the
  running stack or the test database, plus some smaller items. It found no way
  around access control, authentication, CSRF, or the rules against putting
  patient data in logs, errors or URLs. It ran nothing destructive. It left one
  archived "Demo T" patient in your dev database, the same kind of leftover as
  before.

  Findings, most severe first

  #: 1
  Severity: Medium
  Finding: A PUT racing a DELETE brings an archived patient back.
    patient_update saves every column from an object loaded earlier, without
    locking it. If Alice's DELETE (her last follow) commits while her PUT is
    in flight, the PUT writes active=True back. The patient ends up active
    with no followers: nobody can reach it and it's never archived. That's
    exactly what the README says the lock prevents.
  Where: patients/services.py:20
  How it was checked: Interleaving test on the test DB
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Severity: Medium
  Finding: A NUL character in a name gives a 500 HTML page instead of a 400
    OperationOutcome. FHIR's string type allows \u0000, PostgreSQL rejects it.
    Email and phone are already safe.
  Where: patients/fhir.py:213
  How it was checked: curl
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Severity: Low
  Finding: Deeply nested JSON gives a 500 (Python's RecursionError), even
    anonymously on login. Rate-limited, and nothing leaks with DEBUG off, but
    noisy tracebacks in the logs.
  Where: DRF JSONParser
  How it was checked: curl
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Severity: Low
  Finding: Unexpected errors on patient endpoints return Django's HTML 500,
    not an OperationOutcome, which breaks the FHIR error contract. Findings 2
    and 3 are examples.
  Where: patients/views.py:59
  How it was checked: Code + curl
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Severity: Low
  Finding: meta content is silently dropped. meta.security, meta.tag and
    meta.extension are accepted and thrown away, against the AGENTS.md rule
    "rejected, never silently dropped". A client could set a "restricted"
    security label, get a 201, and lose the label without being told.
  Where: patients/fhir.py:31
  How it was checked: curl
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Severity: Low
  Finding: The login rate limit is approximate. DRF's throttle reads then
    writes without locking, so parallel requests can get through: about 3× the
    limit with 3 workers, not unlimited.
  Where: DRF throttle
  How it was checked: Code only, known DRF behaviour
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Severity: Low
  Finding: Missing tests: none for the locks or the archive-vs-update race,
    none for selector scoping at function level, none checking that a 404
    PUT/DELETE leaves the DB unchanged, none for NUL or deep-JSON input.
    There's no test_services.py / test_selectors.py, although AGENTS.md says
    test files mirror source files.
  Where: patients/tests/
  How it was checked: Code
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Severity: Nit
  Finding: <ul><li>An uppercase UUID in the body id is rejected as a mismatch
    (string comparison).</li><li>date.today() instead of
    timezone.localdate().</li><li>Two management commands call the ORM outside
    selectors/services, against AGENTS.md.</li><li>Dockerfile: the app user
    owns the code (could be root-owned), the base image isn't pinned by
    digest, and .dockerignore doesn't exclude .env*/tests/.</li></ul>
  Where:
  How it was checked: Code

  My recommendation

  - Fix: 1, 2, 3+4 together, 5, 7, and the uppercase-UUID nit.
    - #1 is the only one that can corrupt data. The fix: re-load the patient
      with a row lock, scoped to the user, inside the update, and save only the
      changed fields. That also stops a practitioner writing to a patient they
      unfollowed mid-request.
    - #3+4: a parser that turns RecursionError into a 400, a body-size cap, and
      a generic 500 OperationOutcome as the fallback.
  - Document, don't fix: 6. Add it to the README trade-offs; 2FA is already
    listed as the real protection.
  - Your call:
    - Management commands and ORM: move the two calls into accounts/services.py,
      or allow management commands in the AGENTS.md rule. I'd move them, since
      the rule is clearer with no exceptions.
    - Dockerfile hardening: I'd make the code root-owned and extend
      .dockerignore, and skip digest pinning for this project.
    - timezone.localdate(): a one-line change I'd fold in.

  Each fix would start with a failing test. Which ones should I do?
